II. 
Purpose of Testimony 
1 
Q. 
What is the purpose of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel’s                
2 
testimony? 
3 
A. 
The purpose of the Panel’s testimony is to provide a forecast of the capital 
4 
investments of the Company during the 12 months ending December 31, 2017 
5 
(“Rate Year”), the 12 months ending December 31, 2018 (“Data Year 1”) and 
6 
the 12 months ending December 31, 2019 (“Data Year 2”) (Data Year 1 and 
7 
Data Year 2 are collectively referred to as the “Data Years”).  The Panel 
8 
discusses capital expenditures that will (i) increase the safety and reliability of 
9 
the Company’s gas network, (ii) modernize the Company’s gas transmission 
10 
and distribution infrastructure, (iii) promote gas growth in a manner consistent 
11 
with the Commission’s policy objectives and (iv) enhance storm resiliency 
12 
and the Company’s ability to respond to future weather events.  The Panel will 
13 
also discuss the Company’s practices and policies for maximizing the 
14 
efficiency of its capital construction program from planning and budgeting 
15 
through the completion of construction.     
16 
 
17 
 
The Panel’s testimony provides an overview of the significant projects in the 
18 
Company’s capital plan, including acceleration of replacement of leak prone 
19 
pipe (“LPP”) and new safety programs to identify and address system risks.  
20 
The Panel’s testimony also presents an overview of the Company’s pipeline 
21 
11


--- Page 14 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 8 of 128 
integrity and reliability programs that will improve the overall safety and 
1 
reliability of the Company’s gas system, and will also address recently 
2 
enacted, as well as pending, pipeline safety regulations administered by the 
3 
U.S. Department of Transportation (“DOT”), Pipeline and Hazardous 
4 
Materials Safety Administration (“PHMSA”).  Lastly, the Panel discusses the 
5 
Company’s plans to expand gas service to customers through targeted capital 
6 
investments. 
7 
 
8 
Q. 
Does the Panel’s testimony also address the Company’s operations and 
9 
maintenance (“O&M”) programs? 
10 
A. 
Yes.  In addition to capital investments in gas infrastructure, the Panel 
11 
describes a number of O&M programs the Company proposes to expand or 
12 
implement in the Rate Year, the costs of which are not fully reflected in the 
13 
12-month period beginning October 1, 2014 and ending September 30, 2015 
14 
(“Historic Test Year”).  These programs represent known and measureable 
15 
changes from Historic Test Year expense, including programs to (i) improve 
16 
system reliability, (ii) address new and emerging safety regulations, (iii) 
17 
enhance customer service and (iv) support the Company’s capital investments.  
18 
The Panel also discusses various factors contributing to an increase in overall 
19 
O&M costs, such as an increase in the number of gas odor calls and leak 
20 
repairs, additional costs to facilitate customer account transitions and lock 
21 
12


--- Page 15 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 9 of 128 
inactive meters, the cost to install external shut off valves as required by the 
1 
New York City Building Code, higher materials and labor costs, expanded 
2 
municipal paving requirements and increases in permitting fees and penalties.   
3 
  
4 
Q. 
Does the Panel address any other topics? 
5 
A. 
Yes.  The Panel discusses an innovative project developed in partnership with 
6 
the City of New York that turns methane from sewage and organic food waste 
7 
into pipeline quality natural gas, while at the same time reducing emission of 
8 
greenhouse gases (“GHG”).  Lastly, the Panel describes investments in 
9 
information technology, including digital risk and security systems that will 
10 
benefit the Company’s gas business.   
11 
 
12

Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 12 of 128 
III. 
Capital and O&M Plan Priorities and Objectives 
1 
Q. 
Please describe the Company’s overall objective of its infrastructure and 
2 
operations plans. 
3 
A. 
The Company’s gas infrastructure and operations plans are designed to 
4 
provide safe and reliable gas delivery service to customers at reasonable costs.  
5 
Over the last several years, the Company has significantly increased 
6 
investment to modernize and enhance the resiliency of its gas assets.   
7 
Table 1: Historic Capital Investment  
8 
 
9 
Significant capital investment over the next several years is required to ensure 
10 
that the gas system continues to meet the demands of customers.  The 
11 
proposed plan includes capital and O&M spending to meet these needs and to 
12 
satisfy state and federal regulatory requirements and goals, including a 
13 
16


--- Page 19 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 13 of 128 
program to significantly accelerate the replacement of LPP.  In developing its 
1 
capital and O&M plans, the Company balanced the need for spending to 
2 
achieve safety and service objectives with the need to manage costs and 
3 
minimize impacts on customer rates.   
4 
 
5 
Q. 
Why have the Company’s capital expenditures increased over the last 
6 
several years? 
7 
A. 
Several developments have required KEDNY and other natural gas 
8 
distribution utilities to increase their annual capital expenditures.  First, the 
9 
development of new domestic gas sources has created an abundant natural gas 
10 
supply that is conducive to the growth of the gas distribution business from 
11 
both an economic and security/reliability perspective.  Natural gas supplies 
12 
are likely to be available to KEDNY and its customers now and for the 
13 
foreseeable future at a significantly lower cost than the cost to develop 
14 
alternative energy sources.  To take advantage of the favorable gas supply 
15 
dynamics, natural gas utilities are increasing their growth spending to offer the 
16 
economic benefits of relatively inexpensive natural gas supplies to the greatest 
17 
possible number of consumers. 
18 
  
19 
Second, recent pipeline safety incidents, such as the tragic events in San 
20 
Bruno, California, Allentown, Pennsylvania and more recent incidents have 
21 
17


--- Page 20 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 14 of 128 
appropriately increased focus on pipeline safety and the need to carefully 
1 
monitor and replace aging pipeline infrastructure.  
2 
 
3 
Finally, recent weather events such as Superstorm Sandy, Hurricane Irene and 
4 
the Polar Vortex, and the expectation that similar events will continue to 
5 
occur, require the Company to find ways to protect its facilities from severe 
6 
weather.   
7 
 
8 
The foregoing developments indicate that the Company must increase capital 
9 
spending to modernize its transmission and distribution assets, increase the 
10 
size and scope of its safety replacement and reliability programs, and promote 
11 
gas growth.  
12 
 
13 
Q. 
How will the Company support this increased level of capital investment? 
14 
A. 
As the Company developed plans to modernize its gas assets, it also began to 
15 
build and enhance its operations, engineering, resource planning, work 
16 
management and quality control organizations and capabilities to deliver an 
17 
unprecedented level of capital investment.  Over the last several years, the 
18 
Company has hired and trained engineers, designers, planners, estimators, 
19 
project managers, analysts, inspectors and other construction support 
20 
personnel as the investment plan has grown.  The Company will further 
21 
18


--- Page 21 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 15 of 128 
develop these capabilities during CY 2016 and the Rate Year by adding 
1 
incremental resources to execute the capital plan and support the increased 
2 
operations workload (discussed below).  The Company’s efforts to develop its 
3 
internal workforce are discussed by the Human Resources Panel. 
4 
 
5 
With regard to contractor resources, the Company has developed a 
6 
procurement strategy that supports sustainable growth in qualified contractors 
7 
to meet the work plan increases.  To address the overall shortage of qualified, 
8 
skilled labor and the challenges around developing qualified contractors, the 
9 
Company’s resource plan includes the following elements:  
10 
 Establishing longer term contracts to enable contractors to plan and 
11 
invest in hiring, training, facilities and equipment to meet the 
12 
Company’s construction needs;  
13 
 Providing greater work plan visibility to contractors on forecast crew 
14 
requirements to enable them to develop the required capacity; and 
15 
 Managing the work plan to limit seasonal variability to support a 
16 
stable contractor workforce and promote worker retention. 
17 
To encourage portability of the regional contractor workforce, the Company 
18 
recently completed a pilot program, developed in conjunction with the NGA 
19 
and the Gas Technology Institute, to enhance contractor training and 
20 
standardize construction skills.  The Company is also working with 
21 
19


--- Page 22 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 16 of 128 
contractors to develop new pipelines of skilled labor to feed into the 
1 
workforce, including by recruiting prospective utility workers from 
2 
community colleges, trade schools and veteran groups (as discussed in the 
3 
Human Resources Panel’s testimony).   
4 
 
5 
Q. 
Does the Company require additional personnel in the Rate Year and 
6 
Data Years to execute its capital and O&M programs? 
7 
A. 
Yes.  The Company forecasts the need for an additional 199 full time 
8 
equivalent (“FTE”) positions in the Rate Year to support the additional capital 
9 
investment, increasing O&M workload and new programs discussed below.  
10 
These FTEs include positions in field operations, meter services, engineering, 
11 
project management, resource planning, instrumentation and regulation, 
12 
damage prevention, gas production and gas control.   The cost of these FTEs 
13 
will be charged to both capital and O&M programs based on the job function 
14 
and nature of the work.  Exhibit __ (GIOP-6) identifies the incremental FTE 
15 
positions by function and these positions are discussed in more detail below.  
16 
The Company has already begun to fill these positions and will continue to 
17 
hire and train in CY 2016 and into the Rate Year to deliver KEDNY’s capital 
18 
plan and incremental O&M workload.   
19 
20


--- Page 23 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 17 of 128 
A. Pipeline Integrity and Reliability Programs  
1 
i. 
Accelerated LPP Replacement  
2 
Q. 
What is the Company’s proposal regarding the acceleration of its LPP 
3 
replacement program? 
4 
A. 
The Company currently has approximately 1,900 miles of LPP in its inventory 
5 
comprised of: (i) unprotected (i.e., non-cathodically protected) steel pipe 
6 
whether bare or coated and (ii) cast and wrought iron pipe.   
7 
 
8 
To reduce the risk of leaks and breaks, improve system performance and 
9 
reliability, meet the Company’s commitment to enhance customer satisfaction 
10 
and reduce methane emissions, the Company has prioritized the replacement 
11 
of older and higher-risk gas infrastructure – specifically, LPP that 
12 
disproportionally contributes to leaks on KEDNY’s system.  To accelerate 
13

14

15

16 
in LPP retirements of five miles or more in each year following the Rate Year.

Q. 
Why is the Company proposing to accelerate its LPP replacement 
3 
program? 
4 
A. 
The Company’s gas distribution network dates to pre-1900.  Its piping 
5 
inventory consists of cast iron, wrought iron, unprotected bare/coated steel, 
6 
protected steel and plastic.  Based on operating experience, the Company 
7 
anticipates a larger number of failures on leak prone mains as these facilities 
8 
continue to deteriorate over time.  Indeed, as discussed below, the Company 
9 
has observed leak rates for its leak prone mains that are double the average 
10 
leak rate for all Company-owned distribution mains. 
11 
   
12 
Accelerating the rate of main replacements is the best long term approach to 
13 
reducing leaks on the gas system and enhancing system safety.   As shown in 
14 
Table 4 below, the leak rate on LPP is increasing at a rate that exceeds the 
15 
22

leak reductions achieved through the current replacement program.  To 
1 
address this issue, the Company is proposing to increase the replacement rate 
2 
to a mileage level that will reverse the trend.  By accelerating the replacement 
3 
rate to the target schedule, the Company expects to see lower leak rates over 
4 
time – with the inventory of LPP replaced completely in 20 years (as 
5 
compared to more than 40 years at the current rate).  The Company’s 
6

7

8 
0151).

9

11

12

Plan (“DIMP”) and the Company’s Gas Operating Procedure for the 
1

3

4

5

6

9 
Q. 
Please describe the factors contributing to increased main replacement 
10 
costs in KEDNY’s service territory. 
11 
A. 
Several factors are increasing the cost of LPP replacement.  The remaining 
12 
LPP population contains a significant amount of large diameter pipe, which is 
13 
more costly to replace.  The installation of large diameter plastic pipe (larger 
14 
than four inches) calls for fusion joints every 40 feet, requiring a larger layout 
15 
area, larger trenches and more permits as compared to the installation of 
16 
smaller diameter pipe.  Larger pipe is also heavier and requires more 
17 
resources for transportation, handling and installation. 
18 
 
19 
Next, main replacements are costing more in New York City as a result of 
20 
working conditions and more onerous municipal permitting/restoration 
21 
28


--- Page 31 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 25 of 128 
requirements and fees.  Although KEDNY works closely with New York City 
1 
to minimize costs associated with construction (i.e., by coordinating main 
2 
replacements with municipal paving projects), many of these costs are driven 
3 
by field conditions and, therefore, are not within the Company’s control.    
4 
 
5 
Finally, the cost of capital work has increased over the last several years due 
6 
to rising material costs and increased labor and contractor costs because of 
7 
market competition to secure qualified construction resources.   
8 
 
9 
To mitigate the cost, main replacements are coordinated with other programs, 
10 
such as the Public Works, Reinforcement and Reliability programs, to capture 
11 
synergy savings and cost avoidance.  As the Company continues to accelerate 
12 
its rate of LPP replacement, it will look for more of these opportunities to 
13 
deploy construction resources more efficiently.  The Company will also 
14 
identify areas of the gas network where entire LPP systems can be retired 
15 
efficiently and cost effectively.  Finally, KEDNY is implementing a long term 
16 
contractor sourcing strategy (discussed above) to secure qualified construction 
17 
resources capable of performing LPP replacements at reasonable costs. 
18 
  
19

16

17

4 
iii. 
Joint Sealing and Cast Iron Lining 
5 
Q. 
In addition to the LPP replacement programs, is the Company proposing 
6 
any programs to recondition existing main? 
7 
A. 
Yes, the Company’s capital plan includes two programs that utilize pipe lining 
8 
and continuous sealing technologies to cost effectively improve the safety and 
9 
reliability of its large diameter mains.  First, the Company is deploying the 
10 
Cast Iron Joint Sealing Robot or CISBOT.  Second, the Company is utilizing 
11 
cured-in-place (“CIP”) pipe lining to recondition large diameter cast iron and 
12 
steel mains.   
13 
 
14 
Q. 
Please describe the CISBOT and CIP programs. 
15 
A. 
CISBOT is a robotic internal sealing method for cast iron mains 16 inches to 
16 
42 inches in diameter.  As CISBOT moves through the inside of the main, it 
17 
injects a sealant that eliminates existing leaks, prevents future leaks, reduces 
18 
emissions and reconditions cast iron joints, extending the life of the main, 
19 
improving system performance and reducing future joint leaks on treated 
20 
pipeline segments for years.  Unlike traditional construction methods, 
21 
34


--- Page 37 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 31 of 128 
CISBOT allows KEDNY to seal more than 80 joints on a pipeline segment 
1 
from one excavation without taking the main out of service, which avoids 
2 
service disruptions to customers.  This process significantly reduces the 
3 
excavation and paving/restoration costs as compared to conventional leak 
4 
repair methods.  KEDNY is proposing to use CISBOT to address 
5 
approximately two miles of large diameter cast iron main in the Rate Year and 
6 
Data Years. 
7 
 
8 
The CIP pipe lining process installs a treated fabric liner into cast iron and 
9 
steel mains.  An adhesive resin in the liner bonds with the inside wall of the 
10 
pipe, forming a new layer that is impervious to gas.  CIP lining reconditions 
11 
larger diameter cast iron and steel mains, eliminates existing leaks, prevents 
12 
future leaks, reduces emissions, improves performance and extends the life of 
13 
the main for more than 50 years.  CIP lining technology has been used 
14 
extensively on water and sewer facilities in the United States for years and, 
15 
more recently, has been deployed by natural gas utilities to recondition aging 
16 
cast iron and steel mains.  KEDNY is proposing to line 2.5 miles of main in 
17 
the Rate Year and four miles in each of the Data Years.  
18 
 
 
19 
Q. 
How do CISBOT and CIP complement the Company’s LPP replacement 
20 
program? 
21 
35


--- Page 38 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 32 of 128 
A. 
Reconditioning large diameter main with CISBOT and CIP extends the life of 
1 
the main and defers its replacement.  This allows the Company to focus more 
2 
attention, capital investment and construction resources on retiring smaller 
3 
diameter LPP with higher risk profiles, which will enhance the effectiveness 
4 
of KEDNY’s LPP replacement program and promote public safety.   
5 
  
6 
Q. 
What are the cost advantages of CISBOT and CIP lining?    
7 
A. 
Large diameter main replacements and repairs are typically expensive because 
8 
of the location and depth of the excavations required to access the pipe joints.   
9 
The Company’s cost to repair large diameter joints using traditional 
10 
construction methods can range from $15,000 to $30,000 per joint, depending 
11 
on the size and depth of the excavation and paving requirements for each 
12 
individual joint repair.  Using CISBOT, the Company can recondition large 
13 
diameter joints for approximately $5,500 per joint – less than one-third the 
14 
average cost of a traditional joint repair.  This is largely attributable to the fact 
15 
that CISBOT is able to traverse the main and access joints without requiring a 
16 
pit excavation at each joint.       
17 
 
18 
CIP lining is also cost effective and efficient, especially in congested 
19 
metropolitan areas where it is not always possible to locate a sufficient 
20 
subsurface space to install new large diameter main.  Similar to CISBOT, 
21 
36


--- Page 39 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 33 of 128 
because CIP involves less excavation than traditional pipe replacement, it 
1 
reduces construction costs, avoids damage to roads and vegetation, minimizes 
2 
disruptions to the public and provides environmental benefits in the form of 
3 
reduced gas emissions and construction debris while maintaining the safety of 
4 
the main until it can be replaced.   
5 
 
6 
Q. 
What are the Company’s expected capital expenditures on CISBOT and 
7 
CIP? 
8 
A. 
The Company’s capital forecast for these programs includes $14.34 million in 
9 
the Rate Year, $19.28 in Data Year 1 and $19.68 million in Data Year 2.   
10 
 
11 
Q. 
Is the Company proposing a productivity mechanism for its CISBOT and 
12 
CIP programs? 
13 
A. 
Yes.  While CISBOT and CIP are cost effective programs in their own right, 
14 
the Company is proposing to enhance the potential cost benefits to customers 
15 
by including these programs in a productivity pilot that will share any 
16 
CIP/CISBOT cost underruns directly with customers.  To the extent 
17 
KEDNY’s actual costs to deliver the CIP and/or CISBOT annual mileage 
18 
targets are less than the rate allowance for these programs, the Company will 
19 
share the resulting saving with customers.  The Company proposes to retain 
20 
20 percent of any budget underrun as a positive revenue adjustment and return 
21 
37


--- Page 40 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 34 of 128 
80 percent directly to customers.  This mechanism will incent the Company to 
1 
achieve savings in the CISBOT and CIP programs, while at the same time 
2 
providing customers with a direct financial benefit.   The Revenue 
3 
Requirements Panel also discussed the productivity sharing mechanism. 
4 
 
5 
iv. 
Storm Hardening 
6 
Q. 
What is the Company's proposal with respect to storm hardening? 
7 
A. 
The Company maintains approximately 25,000 gas services within the Federal 
8 
Emergency Management Agency’s designated flood zones.  These services 
9 
are especially susceptible to storm surge and flooding that could cause over-
10 
pressurization of the gas facilities connecting customers’ premises.  The 
11 
impacts of recent severe storms (i.e., Superstorm Sandy and Hurricane Irene) 
12 
demonstrate the need for the Company to harden its infrastructure to provide 
13 
greater protection from future storms.   
14 
 
15 
The Company’s storm hardening proposal involves the installation of 
16 
automated service shut-off valves with flood sensors on gas services in flood 
17 
zones.  These valves will operate on a fixed communication network that will 
18 
allow for remote operation and monitoring.  Automated valves stop the flow 
19 
of gas as soon as flooding is detected.  This will prevent regulator over-
20 
pressurization and stop gas from flowing to premises with damaged 
21 
38


--- Page 41 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 35 of 128 
equipment and/or extinguished pilot lights, mitigating the risk of a potential 
1 
incident.  Automated valves also provide a real-time count of services 
2 
impacted by flooding to inform the Company’s storm response about the 
3 
resources needed to restore the affected customers expeditiously.  Lastly, in 
4 
areas where flooding prevents physical access to valves and regulators, remote 
5 
shut-off valves will allow the Company to interrupt only those services 
6 
impacted by flooding, which could spare entire neighborhoods or larger areas 
7 
from losing gas service because of access issues.  
8 
 
9 
The Company’s proposed storm hardening program will deploy 
10 
approximately 25,000 remote shut-off valves in the flood prone areas of 
11 
KEDNY’s system over five years beginning in the Rate Year. 
12 
 
13 
Q. 
What are the Company’s expected capital expenditures for these remote 
14 
valves? 
15 
A. 
The Company’s forecast for this program includes $3.51 million in the Rate 
16 
Year, $4.75 million in Data Year 1 and $4.84 million in Data Year 2.   
17 
 
18 
v. 
Integrity Management and Pending PHMSA Safety Regulations 
19 
Q. 
What is the Company’s Integrity Management Program? 
20 
39


--- Page 42 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 36 of 128 
A. 
The Company’s transmission pipeline Integrity Management Program 
1 
(“IMP”) is a safety program mandated by the Pipeline Safety Improvement 
2 
Act of 2002.  The IMP identifies and addresses potential issues affecting the 
3 
physical soundness of Company facilities before they become safety or 
4 
performance issues.  The Company conducts baseline and periodic 
5 
reassessments of transmission facilities to identify and evaluate potential 
6 
threats to “Covered Segments” of pipelines, i.e., transmission pipelines that 
7 
could affect High Consequence Areas (areas where a pipeline failure could 
8 
have significant adverse consequences), as well as remediation of significant 
9 
defects discovered during such assessments.  Although tests and inspections 
10 
are generally an operating expense, the first inspection run of a pipeline 
11 
segment is capitalized.  Additional capital work is required to support in-line 
12 
inspections (e.g., installation of pig launchers and receivers, and pipe 
13 
reconfiguration/replacement) and to resolve issues discovered during pipeline 
14 
inspections. 
15 
 
16 
KEDNY’s capital investment plan includes IMP investments necessary to 
17 
comply with PHMSA’s current regulations governing transmission pipeline 
18 
integrity management:

Q. 
What is covered in the Company’s Integrity Verification Program 
1 
(“IVP”)? 
2 
A. 
The Pipeline Safety Act of 2011 also mandates that PHMSA establish rules 
3 
requiring operators to demonstrate their pipelines are “fit for service” by 
4 
reviewing construction records for each pipeline segment to confirm it is 
5 
operating within design parameters.  Among the specific changes under 
6 
consideration by PHMSA are new rules regarding the maximum allowable 
7 
operating pressure (“MAOP”) and pressure testing requirements for existing 
8 
pipelines, including (i) eliminating the exemption for establishing the MAOP 
9 
of pre-1970 “grandfathered” pipe segments; (ii) mandating additional pressure 
10 
testing for pipelines previously operating above MAOP; and (iii) requiring 
11 
operators who lack certain records to establish material properties using 
12 
approved methods (e.g., cutting and testing pipe samples).  The final rules are 
13 
expected to be released in 2016.  However, in advance of a final rulemaking, 
14 
PHMSA issued an advisory bulletin (ADB-11-01; January 2011) directing 
15 
operators to perform a detailed threat and risk analysis that includes a records 
16 
review of their systems. 
17 
 
18 
 
Accordingly, the Company is proceeding with an IVP program that includes 
19 
thorough record reviews, pressure tests, pipeline replacement and retirement 
20 
of non-essential pipeline segments.  As with the IMP program, the proposed 
21 
43

IVP program is based on the Company’s assessment of system risks, while 
1 
also incorporating PHMSA’s proposed rulemaking.

Q. 
Please describe some of the technologies and practices the Company uses 
4 
to reduce the total cost of its capital expenditures. 
5 
A. 
The Company continues to employ a number of technologies and best 
6 
practices designed to increase the efficiency and reduce the cost of its capital 
7 
expenditures.  These practices include: 
8 
 Increasing the amount of planned capital work (versus reactive work). 
9 
 Increasing coordination among capital programs to increase 
10 
efficiencies (e.g., leveraging LPP opportunities).  
11 
 Installing more small diameter, high-pressure facilities that can be 
12 
installed at lower cost. 
13 
 Using smaller excavating equipment, increasing operating efficiency 
14 
and reducing instances of damage (because of decreased size and 
15 
weight of equipment). 
16 
 Employing “low dig” technology as opposed to traditional open cut 
17 
methods for main installation, including use of small directional 
18 
drilling machines for services and small diameter mains. 
19 
 Using “coring and keyhole” technology to repair existing mains.  
20 
 Enhancing contractor management.

15

16

17 
released.  Under the Company’s new protocol, a draw down compressor will 
18 
capture the gas (where possible) and inject it into another pipeline that is in 
19 
service.  The Company is currently developing standards and procedures for 
20 
this new blow down process, and anticipates deployment in the Rate Year.

Page 51 of 128 
A. Capital Planning and Budgeting 
1 
Q. 
Please describe the annual development of the Company’s capital plan. 
2 
A. 
Each year, the Company develops a ten-year capital plan to achieve its 
3 
performance objectives of delivering safe, reliable service.  In the summer of 
4 
each year, Investment Planning compiles proposed spending for programs and 
5 
individual capital projects.  Programs and projects are categorized by the four 
6 
spending rationales (Mandated, Growth, Reliability and Non-Infrastructure).  
7 
The proposed spending for each program or project includes the latest cost 
8 
estimates for in-progress projects as well as initial estimates for new projects.  
9 
Expected deviations from historical trends in mix, volume and cost of work 
10 
are considered. 
11 
 
12 
All known mandatory programs and projects are included in the ten-year 
13 
capital plan.  Once the budget level has been established for Mandated work, 
14 
the programs and projects in the other spending rationales are reviewed for 
15 
inclusion in the plan.  Whether any other project is included in the plan is 
16 
based on several factors, including, but not limited to, whether the project is 
17 
new or in-progress, the project risk score and/or resource availability.  In 
18 
addition, program work is examined to capture any possible cost efficiencies, 
19 
specifically with respect to LPP replacement. 
20 
 
21 
55


--- Page 58 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 52 of 128 
In late fall, the capital plan is reviewed by the New York Jurisdictional 
1 
President (Company Witness Kenneth Daly) and the Vice President, Finance, 
2 
New York (Company Witness David Doxsee).  The New York Jurisdictional 
3 
President reviews the overall customer, service quality and financial impacts 
4 
of the investment plan as part of the business planning process and may 
5 
request changes to the level or mix of investments.   
6 
 
7 
In early winter, the capital plan is presented to the National Grid plc Executive 
8 
Committee and, in early spring, the capital portfolio is presented to the 
9 
National Grid plc Board of Directors for review and approval.  
10 
 
11 
Q. 
Are there additional approvals needed before a project in the annual 
12 
capital plan may proceed? 
13 
A. 
Yes.  Aside from the capital planning and budgeting process, specific 
14 
“delegation of authority” approval must be obtained for any project in the ten-
15 
year capital plan to proceed.  Presently, all projects greater than $8 million, 
16 
and all complex projects greater than $1 million, up to a limit of $25 million, 
17 
are reviewed and approved by the U.S. Sanctioning Committee, a committee 
18 
established by the National Grid USA Board of Directors specifically for this 
19 
purpose.  Projects from $1 million to $8 million are approved by the Senior 
20 
Vice President for Gas Process and Engineering.  Projects less than $1 million 
21 
56


--- Page 59 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 53 of 128 
are approved through a supervisory hierarchy based on certain established 
1 
thresholds.  Effective January 1, 2016, projects between $25 million to $176 
2 
million will be reviewed by a newly-established Senior Executive Sanctioning 
3 
Committee.   
4 
 
5 
Q. 
Please describe how the Company’s DIMP impacts its capital investment 
6 
planning. 
7 
A. 
The DIMP involves a risk-based assessment of the Company’s distribution 
8 
system to identify threats in seven categories: corrosion, natural forces, 
9 
excavation damage, other outside force damage, material and weld failure, 
10 
equipment failure/malfunction and inappropriate operation.  The DIMP 
11 
requires evaluation and prioritization of the risks that these threats pose, and 
12 
the implementation of measures to address the highest risks with an emphasis 
13 
on leak management, enhanced damage prevention, operator qualification to 
14 
reduce human error and system replacement.  Consistent with the DIMP, the 
15 
Company prioritizes asset replacements in its investment plan based on a risk 
16 
ranking that considers, among other things, leak repair history, types of leak, 
17 
pipe material, surrounding geography, segment length, nearby construction 
18 
activity, field conditions, customer issues, open leaks and engineering 
19 
judgment.  The risk ranking factors are carefully designed to consider known 
20 
differences in the performance of asset subclasses, extensive experience with 
21 
57


--- Page 60 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 54 of 128 
asset failures, current performance data for the asset subclasses for various 
1 
threat categories, and subject matter experts’ analysis and opinions on the 
2 
future performance of the assets.  
3 
 
 
4

Q. 
Please describe what is included in the City/State construction sub-
1 
category. 
2 
A. 
City/State construction work is performed to accommodate third-party, 
3 
municipal construction activity that could impact the integrity of the 
4 
Company’s natural gas facilities.  Typical third-party construction activities 
5 
that impact gas facilities include work on water, sewer and drainage 
6 
infrastructure, street reconstruction, road realignment and bridge replacement.  
7 
The forecast cost for this program is approximately $197 million in the Rate 
8 
Year.  
9 
 
10 
State regulations and Company procedures require the replacement of eight 
11 
inch and smaller cast iron gas mains if roadway or underground construction 
12 
is being performed in such a way that would impact the integrity of the 
13 
Company’s mains.  Non-cast iron gas mains (i.e., steel and plastic) are not 
14 
subject to the same replacement regulations and are typically supported and 
15 
protected if not in direct conflict with third-party construction.  Direct 
16 
conflicts are addressed through relocation regardless of material type.   
17 
 
18 
KEDNY forecasts its City/State expenditures by reviewing the known and 
19 
planned work identified by municipalities, historical work volumes and unit 
20 
information.   In CY 2015, approximately 169,000 linear feet (approximately 
21 
59


--- Page 62 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 56 of 128 
32 miles) of main replacement was required to address municipal 
1 
infrastructure improvements.  Thirty-four percent ($61 million) of this 
2

21 
63


--- Page 66 ---

and manage the entire project.  If the City’s contractor is qualified by National 
1

2

3

12 
remains to be seen.

Q. 
Please describe New York City Local Law 30. 
11 
A. 
Local Law 30 is a New York City Building Code that requires every gas 
12 
service line in the City to have a gas service valve or other emergency shut-off 
13 
device installed outside.  Local Law 30 sets compliance deadlines for defined 
14 
“Tiers” of gas services.  All Tier 1 gas services (three-family or greater, 
15 
commercial, governmental and industrial) valves were required prior to 
16 
January 1, 2010 and the Company met this requirement.  The Company must 
17 
install all Tier 2 (one and two family homes) valves by January 1, 2020.   
18

5

6

8

9

10

11

12

14 
Q. 
Please describe the Cross Bore Investigation program. 
15 
A. 
A cross bore is an unintended consequence of horizontal directional drilling 
16 
(“HDD”).  It occurs when a plastic gas main goes through a sewer lateral that 
17 
was not identified during the gas installation process.  A cross bore can block 
18 
the sewer line and any attempt to clear the blockage can damage the gas line 
19 
and cause gas to migrate into a building.  Over the last few years, several such 
20 
69


--- Page 72 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 66 of 128 
incidents have occurred in the industry and, as a result, many utilities have 
1 
initiated programs to remedy this situation.   
2 
 
3 
The Company updated its HDD procedures in 2014 to address and eliminate 
4 
possible cross bores.  The proposed investigation and remediation program 
5 
will address all HDD installations prior to 2014 to ascertain if any cross bores 
6 
exist and, if so, to remediate them.  
7 
 
8 
Q. 
What work is addressed in the Latent Damage category? 
9 
A. 
In response to recent industry events and directives coming out of the 
10 
Commission’s generic safety proceedings (including the Horseheads 
11 
Proceeding (11-G-0565) and Plastic Fusions Proceeding (Case 14-G-0212), 
12 
which are discussed in the testimony of the Gas Safety Panel), the Company 
13 
will perform additional inspections of its underground infrastructure when 
14 
opportunities present during the course of normal operations.  For example, 
15 
the Horseheads Proceeding focused attention on the need to inspect facilities 
16 
near third party excavations for potential latent damage.  While the 
17 
Company’s risk assessment did not identify systemic issues with third party 
18 
latent damage, increased inspections will certainly identify facilities in need of 
19 
repair.  Similarly, in the Plastic Fusion Proceeding, the Commission directed 
20 
all LDCs to conduct inspections of plastic fusion joints exposed during regular 
21 
70


--- Page 73 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 67 of 128 
operations.  As some of the plastic facilities on KEDNY’s system are more 
1 
than 40 years old, the Company anticipates these incremental inspections will 
2 
identify the need to replace or repair plastic mains and joints.  The Latent 
3 
Damage category includes an estimate of cost to perform these capital repairs, 
4 
which includes $0.70 million in the Rate Year, $0.81 million in Data Year 1 
5 
and $0.82 million in Data Year 2.

15

16

17

18

19

rationale/category, along with the estimated annual funding during the

2 
 
3 
Q. 
Please describe what is included in the Base Growth category. 
4 
A. 
As discussed in Section III(B) above, Base Growth occurs as a result of new 
5 
construction, customer-initiated conversions and/or gas marketing.  Generally, 
6 
facilities, including main, services and meters, must be installed to serve the 
7 
additional load.  To serve new load economically, the Company must balance 
8 
the costs of adding that load against incremental revenues. 
9 
 
10 
Q. 
Please describe the System Reinforcement category. 
11 
A. 
The System Reinforcement category contains projects intended to ensure that 
12 
minimum system pressures are maintained throughout the gas network during 
13 
periods of peak demand.  The Company models peak demand based on the 
14 
sendout forecasts developed by Analytics, Modeling and Forecasting 
15 
(Company Witness Theodore Poe).  As a result of growth in gas usage in its 
16 
service territory, KEDNY has determined that it is necessary to complete a 
17 
number of projects to ensure its ability to meet peak requirements.  These 
18 
reinforcement projects are essential to serve growing demand, particularly in 
19 
the constrained area of Northern Queens.   
20 
 
21 
72


--- Page 75 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 69 of 128 
During the winter of 2014/2015, KEDNY recorded seven of its top ten 
1 
sendout records, including a firm load record of 1,331,796 dekatherms on 
2 
February 15, 2015 when the average temperature was nine degrees Fahrenheit. 
3 
The recent growth in peak sendout underscores the need to ensure that 
4 
minimum system design pressures are maintained throughout the distribution 
5 
network during periods of peak demand. 
6 
 
7 
Q. 
Please provide examples of System Reinforcement projects. 
8 
A. 
Examples of System Reinforcement projects include: 
9 
 Replacing undersized mains with larger diameter mains.  LPP is 
10 
targeted whenever practical during this work. 
11 
 Looping or connecting system endpoints by installing new main. 
12 
 System pressure uprates (e.g., 15 pounds per square inch (“psi”) to 60 
13 
psi). 
14 
 Installing new district regulators and replacing existing undersized 
15 
district regulators. 
16 
 Transferring existing low pressure customers to an adjacent high-
17 
pressure main (i.e., load shedding). 
18

21 
74


--- Page 77 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 71 of 128 
Q. 
Please describe what is included in the Heater Program category. 
1 
A. 
There are 40 natural gas heaters currently operating on the Company’s system.  
2 
Because high-pressure gas cools when reduced to a lower pressure, heaters are 
3 
required at pressure regulating stations to prevent freeze-ups that can impact 
4 
flow control devices.  In addition, cold gas temperatures can lead to reduced 
5 
pipe toughness and increased potential for frost heave and cold temperature-
6 
induced stresses.  The heater program adds new heaters (where required) and 
7 
replaces or rebuilds existing heaters that have reached the end of their useful 
8 
lives or require component replacement. 
9 
 
10 
Q. 
Please describe what is included in the I&R Reactive category. 
11 
A. 
The reactive I&R budget provides funding for capital investment in pressure 
12 
regulating and control stations.  Typical projects in this category include 
13 
unplanned capital work resulting from emergency conditions, including the 
14 
replacement of station valves, regulators and relief valves, as well as related 
15 
capital work on station equipment. 
16 
 
17 
Q. 
Please describe what is included in the Pressure Regulating Facilities 
18 
category. 
19 
A. 
The Pressure Regulating Facilities category provides funding for replacement 
20 
and/or rebuilding and reconditioning of existing regulating and control 
21 
75


--- Page 78 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 72 of 128 
stations.  Pressure regulating facilities (or stations) are designed to control 
1 
system pressures and maintain continuity of supply during normal operating 
2 
conditions and during periods of peak gas demand.  This category includes 
3 
full or partial replacement of existing stations.   
4 
 
5 
KEDNY has assessed regulating stations on its system, evaluating factors 
6 
such as pressure, location and the number of dependent customers for each 
7 
station.  In addition, the assessment considered station condition, including 
8 
pipe corrosion, location and type of overpressure protection, automation, 
9 
condition of vaults, vault covers, wall sleeves, piping vents and ladders.  The 
10 
results of the assessment were used to create an overall risk rating for each 
11 
station that serves as the basis for prioritizing projects in this area. 
12 
 
13 
Q. 
Please describe what is included in the Remote Controlled Valve (“RCV”) 
14 
category. 
15 
A. 
This program involves the installation of additional RCVs on transmission 
16 
pipelines to improve emergency response capability and reduce the risk of gas 
17 
releases.  In the event of a pipeline failure, RCVs allow control room 
18 
operators to stop the flow of gas and isolate and shut down a portion of the 
19 
system remotely.  Currently, transmission pipelines can only be shut down 
20 
using manually-controlled isolation valves, which can take longer to close and 
21 
76


--- Page 79 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 73 of 128 
result in a larger number of customers being isolated.  Improving response 
1 
time through the expanded deployment of RCVs reduces the quantity of gas 
2 
released and reduces the likelihood of harm to people and property.   
3 
 
4 
Q. 
Why does the Company propose to make this investment in the Rate 
5 
Year? 
6 
A. 
The PHMSA regulations promulgated in response to the Pipeline Safety Act 
7 
of 2011 will mandate the installation of additional RCVs.  But even in the 
8 
absence of the PHMSA regulations, investment in RCVs is required given the 
9 
safety and reliability benefits.  As highlighted by recent industry events, there 
10 
are significant operational benefits associated with the increased deployment 
11 
of RCVs, such as enhanced pipeline shutdown capabilities. 
12 
 
13 
Q. 
Please describe what is included in the Valve Installation and 
14 
Replacement category. 
15 
A. 
Federal and state regulations require installation, inspection, operation and 
16 
maintenance of critical pipeline valves on all gas distribution systems.  The 
17 
purpose of these valves is to facilitate the rapid shutdown of distribution 
18 
piping during gas emergencies such as third-party damage or water intrusion.  
19 
A secondary purpose of these valves is to facilitate maintenance and pipe 
20 
replacement on associated distribution piping.   
21 
77


--- Page 80 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 74 of 128 
This program will strengthen the Company’s emergency response capabilities 
1 
by improving the level at which Field Operations personnel can safely and 
2 
efficiently isolate sections of the distribution system while mitigating 
3 
customer impacts (e.g., reducing the duration of future outages).  Ensuring all 
4 
critical valves are properly maintained and operable is a key public safety 
5 
function and is essential to the effective operation of the Company’s gas 
6 
distribution system. 
7 
 
8 
Q. 
Please describe what is included in the Water Intrusion category. 
9 
A. 
The Water Intrusion program is designed to address water entering the gas 
10 
distribution system, resulting in main obstructions, poor pressure and/or 
11 
freezing customer services.  This program targets the retirement of LPP that is 
12 
susceptible to water intrusion but is not prioritized for replacement under the 
13 
Main Replacement programs because of the absence of leaks and/or historical 
14 
leak repair activity. 
15 
 
16 
Q. 
What are the goals of the Water Intrusion program? 
17 
A. 
The goals of the Water Intrusion program include improved customer 
18 
satisfaction by focusing on addressing reliability and integrity issues.  Also, 
19 
the program will support the continued elimination of low pressure 
20 
distribution systems by elevating pressure whenever practical.  Finally, 
21 
78


--- Page 81 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 75 of 128 
successful execution of the program will promote public and municipal 
1 
relations as fewer unplanned outages will reduce unplanned road excavations. 
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Please describe what is included in the Control Line Integrity category. 
4 
A. 
Control line piping is small diameter (two inches or less) piping used to 
5 
monitor and control the pressures and flows at pressure regulating facilities.  
6 
Control lines provide pressure feedback to the regulators and system 
7 
automation equipment within the station.  They are critical to maintaining 
8 
control of system pressures and to maintaining continuity of supply during 
9 
periods of normal and peak gas demand.  The key driver for the Control Line 
10 
Integrity program is the remediation or replacement of control line piping at 
11 
pressure regulator stations that do not meet current standards of reliability, 
12 
safety and performance.  The program is designed to assess, remediate and/or 
13 
replace control lines with these issues.  Field data on control line conditions is 
14 
collected during annual regulator station testing and on-site inspections.  
15 
Based on the results of those inspections, projects in this area are coordinated 
16 
with the proactive regulator program. 
17 
 
18 
Q. 
Please describe what is included in the Gas Planning category. 
19 
79


--- Page 82 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 76 of 128 
A. 
The Gas Planning program ensures that customers continue to have reliable 
1 
service and that no customers experience interruptions as a result of an 
2 
unplanned outage of a facility under normal winter conditions.   
3 
 
4 
Q. 
Please provide examples of Gas Planning projects. 
5 
A. 
Examples of Gas Planning projects include: eliminating distribution systems 
6 
fed by a single district regulator or main, integrating distribution systems with 
7 
the same operating pressures through pipeline connections, adding new supply 
8 
diversity, and projects targeting areas of the system where large numbers of 
9 
customers would lose service if a gas facility became inoperable when the 
10 
average daily temperature is 15 degrees Fahrenheit. 
11 
 
12 
Q. 
Please describe what is included in the System Automation category. 
13 
A. 
This program will install Remote Terminal Units (“RTUs”) at multiple gate 
14 
and regulator stations.  RTUs provide temperature, pressure and flow data 
15 
back to the Gas Control Room.  RTUs can also monitor gas detectors and 
16 
intrusion alarms and allow Gas Control Operators to adjust flow and pressure 
17 
set points at regulator stations.  The benefits include enhanced calibration of 
18 
network models from automation and telemetry data, improved accuracy of 
19 
network analysis, and enhanced ability to forecast the need for capital 
20 
reinforcements, which will lead to more efficient capital planning.  
21 
80


--- Page 83 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 77 of 128 
Automation allows Gas Control Operators to selectively close valves, raise or 
1 
lower pressures, and shut down take stations.  System alarms also alert Gas 
2 
Control Operators to system issues and allow quick pinpointing of the source.  
3 
 
4 
PHMSA regulations regarding Control Room Management require Operators 
5 
to ensure that “practices and procedures within their control rooms are 
6 
adequate to maintain pipeline safety and integrity.”  These rules indicate that 
7 
Operators should have telemetry to monitor pipelines, as it would increase 
8 
system awareness and enable a proactive response to abnormal operating 
9 
conditions.  The System Automation program complies with these regulations 
10 
by providing for increased deployment of telemetry on the Company’s system. 
11 
 
12 
Q. 
How is system performance monitored currently? 
13 
A. 
Currently, 58 percent of the pressure regulation stations are equipped with 
14 
some form of telemetry, while the rest of the system relies on paper chart 
15 
recorders.  KEDNY’s System Automation program adds automation 
16 
capabilities to low pressure regulator stations.  RTUs installed under the 
17 
System Automation program will provide enhanced ability to monitor system 
18 
performance and remotely adjust pressures on the gas system.  The program 
19 
will also replace aging and obsolete telemetry equipment. 
20 
 
21 
81


--- Page 84 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 78 of 128 
Q. 
Please describe the Company’s LNG program. 
1 
A. 
The Company maintains on-system supply through its LNG facility at the 
2 
Greenpoint LNG Plant.  The Greenpoint LNG Tank #1 (0.6 billion cubic feet 
3 
(“bcf”)) was put into service in 1968 and Tank #2 (one bcf) was put into 
4 
service in 1971.  The Greenpoint LNG Plant is capable of supplying 290 
5 
million cubic feet of gas per day, which represents approximately 18 percent 
6 
of KEDNY’s peak day demand.  Refilling the tanks is accomplished through 
7 
liquefaction during the summer period when gas supplies are available and 
8 
less expensive.  The liquefaction system can refill at a rate of about 7 to 8.5 
9 
million cubic feet of gas per day and it takes between 150 and 200 days to 
10 
refill both tanks. 
11 
 
12 
 
Noting the critical roles LNG plays in meeting peak demand, the Commission 
13 
recently directed KEDNY to prioritize spending at the Greenpoint LNG Plant 
14 
to enhance storm resiliency and avoid disruptions to LNG production. 
15 
 
16

17

The Greenpoint LNG Plant has been in service for more than 40 years and 
19 
requires significant investment to support continued safe and reliable

operation.  Therefore, the Company proposes a number of capital projects for

Q. 
What is the Tank Modernization Project? 
12 
A.

13 
the tank to perform a major tank upgrade.  Specifically, the project scope 
14 
includes: (i) eliminating the bottom penetration nozzles on the tank, (ii) 
15

16 
systems on the tank to bring them up to current code, (iv) replacing the tank 
17 
foundation heaters and (v) performing detailed non-destructive examination of 
18 
tank plate welds to validate long term integrity.

20 
Q. 
Please described the proposal for the Salt Water Pump House. 
21 
84


--- Page 87 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 81 of 128 
A. 
The Company is proposing to construct a new Salt Water Pump House that 
1 
will provide a dedicated source of salt water to supply the deluge system for 
2 
each tank.  The deluge system consists of piping and nozzles that surround 
3 
each tank and produce a 60 to 70 foot high water curtain to protect the plant in 
4 
the event of an LNG spill.  The water curtain requires approximately 12,000 
5 
gallons of water per minute.  The current pump house system at the plant is at 
6 
the end of its useful life and requires replacement.  The new Salt Water Pump 
7 
House will more effectively provide adequate supplies of salt water to support 
8 
operation of the deluge system.  
9

Q. 
Please describe the Spring Creek Reconfiguration and Reinforcement 
11 
Project. 
12 
A. 
The Spring Creek Reconfiguration and Reinforcement Project is required to 
13

14 
well as to address integrity issues within the Spring Creek Gate Station and 
15 
IF5 Regulator Station.  The project involves installation of two new regulator 
16 
runs at the Spring Creek Gate Station that will feed the 15 psig system.  The 
17 
two new runs will include approximately 1,100 feet of 30 inch steel main.  
18 
This will allow KEDNY to retire the IF5 Regulator Station rather than spend 
19 
considerable funds to renovate it.  The retirement of the IF5 Station has the 
20 
added benefit of being able to retire approximately 210 feet of main that is 
21 
85


--- Page 88 ---

currently undersized and would need to be replaced even if IF5 Station 
1 
remained in service.  An additional component of this project will involve 
2 
transferring low pressure Governor 88, which is fed by the 15 psig system, to 
3 
the 60 psig system thereby improving pressures on the 15 psig pressure 
4 
system.

6 
Q. 
Please describe the Metropolitan Reliability Infrastructure (“MRI”) 
7 
Project. 
8 
A. 
The MRI project will provide the downstate New York system with increased 
9 
supply diversity, pressure support, outage contingency, operational flexibility 
10 
and operational autonomy by reducing dependence on gas from Consolidated 
11 
Edison Company of New York (“Con Edison”) at Newtown Creek.   The 
12 
project will also maximize existing and proposed upstream capacity that will 
13 
better position the Company to meet customers’ long-term supply needs over 
14 
the next 15 to 20 years.  
15 
 
16 
Specifically, the MRI project will provide an operational loop to the Brooklyn 
17 
backbone system consisting of approximately 34,000 feet of 30 inch, 350 psig 
18 
transmission main from Linden Boulevard in Brownsville to Maspeth Avenue 
19 
in Greenpoint (and an associated gate station).  This new loop will: 
20 
86


--- Page 89 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 83 of 128 
 Immediately improve reliability by increasing supply sources.  Sources 
1 
from any directly connected supply point will be accessible and gas 
2 
may be moved to any point across the Company’s system.  This will 
3 
significantly reduce the reliance on any individual gate deliveries. 
4 
 Allow the Brooklyn backbone system to be taken out of service for 
5 
system integrity work, while still maintaining gas operations. 
6 
 Enhance the effectiveness of RCVs on the transmission system by 
7 
allowing increased utilization of remote valves in the event of a system 
8 
disturbance that requires a remote shut down (without disrupting 
9 
service to potentially thousands of customers). 
10 
 Reinforce the Brooklyn/Queens high pressure system by installing a 
11 
distribution regulator station, which will also facilitate the retirement 
12 
of LPP by replacing lower pressure cast iron mains with high pressure 
13 
plastic. 
14 
 Provide for the movement of incremental supplies from both the 
15 
Narrows and the Lower New York Bay Lateral to any point across the 
16 
system, including transfer points with Con Edison.    
17 
The MRI Project will enable KEDNY to move an additional 850 dekatherms 
18 
each day by 2021.  The forecast for MRI project is $45.46 million in the Rate 
19 
Year, $86.54 million in Data Year 1 and $56.72 million in Data Year 2.  The

1 
Q.

2

3 
A.

4

5

6

7 
KEDNY's service territory will be complete in CY 2018.  
8 
 
9 
Q. 
Is additional investment required after full AMR deployment has been 
10 
achieved? 
11 
A. 
Yes.  KEDNY has utilized AMR in its service territory for nearly twenty years, 
12 
thus a significant number of existing AMR units are at or near the end of their 
13 
useful lives.  As a result, additional capital costs are required to replace 
14 
existing AMR units and batteries.       
15 
 
16 
Q. 
What are the customer benefits of AMR? 
17 
A. 
The installation of AMRs will significantly decrease the number of estimated 
18 
bills.  Additionally, AMRs enhance storm resiliency and improve customer 
19 
service.  In the aftermath of major storms, meters usually go unread because 
20 
meter-reading personnel are needed to assist with storm recovery.  AMRs will 
21 
89

Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel

allow the Company to continuing gathering gas usage data following major 
1

iv. 
Capitalization Policy Change – Corrosion 
1 
Q. 
Is the Company proposing any changes to its capitalization policy for 
2 
corrosion control activities? 
3 
A. 
Yes.  Accounting for some corrosion control activities is currently expensed 
4 
by the Company.  However, applicable accounting principles and regulations 
5 
permit the installation/replacement of new test stations and rectifiers, among 
6 
other items, to be capitalized.  The Company is proposing to capitalize these 
7 
corrosion control activities in accordance with Accounting Standards 
8 
Codification 360, the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission’s accounting 
9 
regulations and International Accounting Standard 16.  This change will 
10 
standardize the accounting treatment of these work items between National 
11 
Grid’s downstate New York gas distribution companies.  Testing and 
12 
inspection activities related to corrosion control will remain as expensed 
13 
items.

Q.

Q. 
Please generally describe the nature of the Company’s gas system O&M 
9 
expenses. 
10 
A. 
O&M expenses relate to work performed to provide customer support, 
11 
respond to emergencies, perform safety inspections and other compliance 
12 
activities, restore service and maintain the life of capital assets.  The Company 
13 
has a significant maintenance program to ensure that system assets are utilized 
14 
to their fullest potential life expectancy.  As gas facilities age, maintenance 
15 
costs increase.  These costs include costs for more frequent inspection and 
16 
testing, increased volume of repairs, more significant repair work and 
17 
93


--- Page 96 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 90 of 128 
increased emergency work.  These expenditures are required to prevent failure 
1 
and maintain the life of the assets until replacement occurs. 
2 
 
3 
 
The Company’s O&M programs are also designed to maintain the service 
4 
commitments in its gas safety performance metrics, which cover various 
5 
aspects of its performance in the areas of reliability and safety, including 
6 
metrics measuring pipeline replacement, emergency response, leak 
7 
management and damage prevention.  Since these performance measures were 
8 
established, the Company has consistently met or exceeded performance 
9 
targets.   
10

Year.

7 
Q. 
Please describe how the Company manages leak repairs and surveillance. 
8 
A. 
CMS is responsible for leak response, surveillance and inside repairs.  Field 
9 
Operations is responsible for repairs on mains and services and leak surveys.  
10 
Leak survey and surveillance includes completion of all transmission and 
11 
distribution mandated leak survey and system patrols to ensure the integrity of 
12 
the gas system.     
13 
 
14 
 
Over the last several years, KEDNY has seen an increase in its leak 
15

16 
which require repair and/or inspection at designated intervals depending on 
17 
the leak classification (i.e., Type 1, 2, 2A, 3).  This increase in the number of 
18 
detected leaks is attributable to a combination of the new FDNY gas 
19 
emergency procedures and increased public awareness on gas safety driving 
20 
more leak calls, higher leak rates on the Company’s LPP and colder weather.  
21 
99

As a result, KEDNY has experienced an increase in its leak surveillance 
1

2 
Company is performing additional leak repairs to address the increased 
3 
volume of new leaks and to reduce its backlog of non-hazardous leaks.

vi. 
Pressure Testing 
17 
Q. 
Please describe the increased O&M expense to perform pressure testing 
18 
on new pipeline segments.  
19 
A. 
In 2015, the Commission amended its regulations (16 NYCRR § 255.507) to 
20 
eliminate the option of soap testing short sections (100 feet or less) of gas 
21 
102


--- Page 105 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 99 of 128 
piping before it is placed into service.  As result, the Company must now 
1 
perform a pressure test on each segment of new pipe, which requires that the 
2 
field crew weld caps on each end of the new pipe segment, pressurize the pipe 
3 
and confirm that the pipe maintains pressure for an hour or more.  The new 
4 
pressure testing requirement adds several man hours to the typical leak repair.  
5 
As a result of this change in process, the Company estimates incremental 
6 
O&M costs of $0.205 million in the Rate Year.

i. 
Enhanced Damage Prevention 
13 
Q. 
Please describe the nature and impact of the Company’s efforts to 
14 
promote damage prevention as a cost-containment strategy.  
15 
109


--- Page 112 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 106 of 128 
A.  
The Company is focusing new attention on its damage prevention program in 
1 
an effort to reduce instances of third-party damage.  Third-party damage not 
2 
only raises public safety concerns, but it also increases the costs of 
3 
maintaining the distribution system because the Company has to remediate, 
4 
repair or replace its facilities that have been damaged and Company resources 
5 
have to be devoted to these activities on an unplanned basis.  
6 
 
7 
To reduce the potential for third-party damage and the resulting public safety 
8 
and cost impacts, over the last several years the Company has invested in 
9 
improvements to training and education of third-party excavators.  Also, the 
10 
Company has stepped up its communications with third-party excavators and 
11 
has instituted new communication protocols with municipalities regarding 
12 
permitting and construction activities. 
13 
 
14 
Q. 
Please describe the Company’s proposal to reduce instances of third-
15 
party damage. 
16 
A. 
The Company’s plan is to drive improvement in the area of damage 
17 
prevention by focusing on the most frequent cause of third-party damage, 
18 
which is excavator error.  Accordingly, the Company is proposing a Damage 
19 
Prevention Advisor program based on a similar program adopted by NMPC.  
20 
This program would consist of five Damage Prevention Advisors, who would 
21 
110


--- Page 113 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 107 of 128 
be equipped with vehicles and equipment that allow employees/contractors to 
1 
survey the ticket management systems for active location requests and 
2 
proactively work with excavators to reduce instances of damage.   
3 
 
4 
Q. 
Please explain in more detail how adding five Damage Prevention 
5 
Advisors would help lower instances of third-party damage.  
6 
A. 
Of the 263 instances of third-party damage recorded in CY 2014, 157 were the 
7 
result of excavator errors.  While it is difficult to quantify the expected impact 
8 
of the Damage Prevention Advisors on instances of third-party damage, the 
9 
program is specifically targeted at lowering these excavator errors.  These 
10 
dedicated damage prevention employees would visit excavation sites with 
11 
active One Call tickets to: 
12 
 Remind excavation crews of the requirements included in the New 
13 
York State Damage Prevention regulations to improve compliance. 
14 
 Remind excavators to verify and expose facilities via hand digging, as 
15 
required. 
16 
 Discuss appropriate steps to take if facilities are not located. 
17 
 Provide safe digging literature. 
18 
 Coordinate requests for safe digging training.  
19 
111


--- Page 114 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 108 of 128 
The Damage Prevention Advisors would also stay on site with excavators 
1 
with repeated errors during excavation and investigate sites where there is no 
2 
location request.  
3 
 
4 
Q. 
Is the Company’s proposed Damage Prevention program supported by 
5 
the findings in the Commission’s recent Horseheads proceeding?  
6 
A. 
Yes.  In the Horseheads proceeding, the Commission directed New York’s 
7 
LDCs to perform risk assessments of their gas systems to identify instances of 
8 
latent damage to underground gas facilities caused by third party excavations.  
9 
The Company’s response to the Horseheads proceeding is described in the 
10 
Gas Safety Panel’s testimony.  While KEDNY’s risk assessment did not 
11 
identify significant instances of latent damage on the gas system, the 
12 
Company believes it is prudent to enhance inspections of municipal 
13 
infrastructure work near gas facilities.  Accordingly, the Company’s damage 
14 
prevention program will include (i) proactive inspections of municipal 
15 
infrastructure projects and (ii) increased municipal and public outreach in its 
16 
service territory.   
17
